26:["$","p",null,{"children":["Rusty's wheels are its only sense of motion. Each encoder counts the fraction of a revolution its\nwheel has turned; multiply by the wheel radius and you get how far that wheel ",["$","em",null,{"children":"rolled"}],". Integrate\nboth, and you have a pose. This is ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"dead reckoning"}],", and it is the oldest navigation algorithm\nthere is."]}]
27:["$","p",null,{"children":["It also has a defect that no amount of engineering removes. An encoder measures the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"wheel"}],". It\ndoes not measure the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"floor"}],". Every time a wheel slips a millimetre, skids on a threshold, or\nturns out to be 0.3 mm larger than the CAD model says, the number the encoder reports and the\ndistance the robot travelled part company — and because dead reckoning integrates, that\ndisagreement is never corrected, only accumulated."]}]

2a:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The error grows and never shrinks."}]," There is no mechanism in dead reckoning that could shrink\nit. A measurement of the outside world can pull an estimate back toward the truth; a measurement of\nyour own wheels cannot, because it carries no information about where you started."]}]
2b:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Turns are more expensive than straights."}]," A heading error is nearly free while you stand still\nand catastrophic once you drive under it: three degrees of heading error becomes half a metre of\nposition error after ten metres of driving. This is why the \"drift / distance\" readout jumps at\neach end of the corridor."]}]
2c:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"With slip set to zero, the two traces coincide exactly."}]," That is worth sitting with. It means\nthe drift you just watched is not a property of dead reckoning as an ",["$","em",null,{"children":"idea"}]," — the arithmetic is\nexact — but of the physics the arithmetic is being fed. Which raises the obvious question: why can\nno real robot set that slider to zero? Two reasons, and they behave completely differently. Drag\nthe second slider and watch what a one-percent error in one wheel radius does."]}]

30:["$","p",null,{"children":["Sensors split along two axes that matter more than any device catalog. f2:["$","p",null,{"children":["Read the last column. A one-percent error in one wheel radius produces six times the drift of quite\naggressive slip — and produces ",["$","em",null,{"children":"exactly"}]," the same number on every seed, because it is not noise. No\nfilter removes it, no amount of averaging cancels it, and running your benchmark over a thousand\nseeds will not reveal it. You calibrate it, or you estimate it as part of the state\n(",["$","$L1d",null,{"href":"/chapters/ch14-ekf-slam","children":"Chapter 14"}]," does exactly this for the map; the same trick works for\nwheel radii)."]}]
